පාර්ලිගේන්තතුව

ගු ආනන්ද විවේපාල මතතා (මතජන ආරක්ෂානක සත පාර්තලිවම්න්ු ක යුු අමාතයුමා) (மொண்புமிகு ஆனந்த ெியஜபொல - தபொதுமக்கள் பொதுகொப்பு மற்றும் பொரொளுமன்ற அலுெல்கள் அமமச்சர்) (The Hon. Ananda Wijepala - Minister of Public Security and Parliamentary Affairs) ගරු කථානායකතුමනි ගරු කාදර්ල මසේ ාන්ත මන්තීතුමා ිතිදන්ත සේථාවර නිගයෝග 27(2) යටගත් ගමම ‍රශේනය ගයාුල කර තිගබනවා. 2008 අ භයන්ත ර පියපාලන අමා යාචශග ා‍රගල්ඛ්‍යක්ෂ පදනේ කර ගනිමින්ත සහ 2016 අභයන්ත ර කටයුතු වයඹ සචවර්ලධන සචසේක්තික කටයුතු අමා යාචශග ා‍රගල්ඛ්‍යක්ෂ පදනේ කර ගනිමින්ත ආගමික නායකයින්ත සඳහා නිල ගමන්ත බලපත්‍ර නිකුත් කිරීම සේබන්තධව සහ ඔවුන්ත පිනචචි ිනසේත්‍රික්ෂක ඔවුන්ත ිය සචාාර පිළිබඳව ජර්ලඝ ූර්ලය ිතසේ රයක්ෂ ලබා ගදන්තන කියලා මයි ගේ ‍රශේනගයන්ත ඉල්ලා තිගබන්තගන්ත. එම ග ාරතුරු ඉ ා ගකටි කාලයකින්ත ලබා ගතනීමට අපහන්යි ගරු මන්තීතුමනි.

ගරු කථානායකතුමනි සති ගදකක කාලයක්ෂ තුළ ගේකට පිළිතුරක්ෂ ලබා ගදන්තන උත්සාහ කරන්තනේ. ගමාකද මීට අදාළව ග ාරතුරු ිතශාල ‍රමායයක්ෂ ඒක රාශි කර ගන්තන අවශය වනවා. ගබාගහාම සේතුතියි.

ගු වක්. කාදර්ත මසනතාන් මතතා (மொண்புமிகு யக. கொதர் மஸ்தொன்) (The Hon. K. Kader Masthan) Sir, I rise to a point of Order. ගු කථානායකුමා (மொண்புமிகு சபொநொயகர் அெர்கள்) (The Hon. Speaker) ගරු ගක්ෂ. කාදර්ල මසේ ාන්ත මන්තීතුමා. ගු වක්. කාදර්ත මසනතාන් මතතා (மொண்புமிகு யக. கொதர் மஸ்தொன்) (The Hon. K. Kader Masthan) தகௌரெ அமமச்சர் அெர்கயள, நீங்கள் பதில் ெழங்கும் தபொழுது, இெர்களுக்கு எந்த criteriaஇன் அடிப்பமடயில் passport ெழங்கப்பட்டுள்ளது என்ற ெிபரத்மதயும் யசர்த்து ெழங்குமொறு யகட்டுக் தகொள்கின்யறன்.

ගු ආනන්ද විවේපාල මතතා (மொண்புமிகு ஆனந்த ெியஜபொல) (The Hon. Ananda Wijepala) ගරු කථානායකතුමනි ඒ criteria පන්ිය ආඩුක් ිතිදන්ත අමා ය මඩුකයලගයන්ත ගහෝ යේ යේ ා‍රගල්ඛ්‍ ම අනුම කරපු ඒවා. ඒ ිතසේ ර ිතශාල ‍රමායයක්ෂ තිගබනවා. ඒ ිදයල්ල ලබා ගදනවා. ඒකට සාධාරය කාලයක්ෂ ලබා ගදන්තන කියලා මයි ඉල්ලා ිදටින්තගන්ත. සති ගදකක්ෂ ඇතුළ පිළිතුරු ලබා ගදන්තන උත්සාහ කරනවා. එගහම බතිය වුගයාත් වත් සතියකවත් සාධාරය කාලයක්ෂ ලබා ගදන්තන. ගමාකද ගේ සේබන්තධගයන්ත ජර්ලඝ පිළිතුරක්ෂ ලබා ගදන්තන ගවයි. මම ිඳ න ිතිකයට පිටු ගදතුන්තිදයයකට වතජි පිළිතුරක්ෂ ගේ තුළ තිගබනවා.

ගු කථානායකුමා (மொண்புமிகு சபொநொயகர் அெர்கள்) (The Hon. Speaker) සේථාවර නිගයෝග 27(2) යටගත් මීළඟ ‍රශේනය ගරු ෂායක්ෂකියන්ත රායපුත්තිරන්ත රාසමාණික්ෂකේ මන්තීතුමා.

II

සත යය, වගීම සත සංිඳියාව සතතික කිරීම සඳතා

රජවේ මුලිළරීම உண்மம, தபொறுப்புக்கூறல் மற்றும் நல்லிணக்கத்மத

உறுதி தசய்ெதில் அரசொங்கத்தின் முன்முயற்சி

GOVERNMENT’S INITIATIVE ON ENSURING TRUTH,

ACCOUNTABILITY AND RECONCILIATION

ගු ෂානාණක්කියන් රාජපු් තිරන් රාසමාණික්කම් මතතා (மொண்புமிகு சொணக்கியன் ரொஜபுத்திரன் இரொசமொணிக்கம்) (The Hon. Shanakiyan Rajaputhiran Rasamanickam) Hon. Speaker, in light of the UN Human Rights Council 61: Sri Lanka Core Group Statement, which calls for credible accountability, strong independent institutions and meaningful engagement with OHCHR, and noting that victim communities, particularly families of the disappeared, have rejected domestic mechanisms and continue to call for credible international investigations, I ask the Hon. Minister of Justice and National Integration the following questions.

Further, while previous Governments, at least, engaged in discussions on truth, reconciliation and accountability mechanisms, the present Government’s policy framework focuses on governance and anti-corruption without clearly addressing war-time accountability and justice. Hon. Minister of Justice, the mandate you got was not only to deal with corruption, but also to address accountability and justice issues.

1. What is the Government’s current official estimate of persons missing during the final stages of the armed conflict in 2009 and has that been updated?

2. Does the Government accept that there is still no credible consolidated database of missing persons and what steps are being taken to establish one?

3. What is the status of investigations into the Chemmani mass graves discovered in 2025 and the timeline for forensic analysis, identification and accountability?

4. How many mass grave sites have been officially identified to date and what progress has been made in those investigations?

5. Will the Government permit or seek international forensic and investigative assistance into these cases?

6. What is the Government’s position on individuals who allegedly surrendered to the military in the final days of the war and are now missing?

7. Will the Government utilize evidence compiled by the Sri Lanka Accountability Project - SLAP - in pursuing such cases?

8. What outcomes has the Office on Missing Persons delivered since this Government assumed office?

9. What actions has the Office for Reparations taken and how does the Government respond to concerns that reparations without truth and justice are insufficient?

2521 2522

2026 මාර්ලතු 20

10. Finally, will the Government commit to a time-bound credible process, including international involvement, to deliver accountability and non-recurrence?

Hon. Minister, I am asking you these questions in light of your President having been in power for a year and six months now and most of these concerns have still not been addressed. I have asked the Hon. Prime Minister two such questions previously under Questions posed to Hon. Prime Minister, and she also confirmed that it is work in progress. But, we are yet to see any concrete steps; as Representatives of the victim community, we are unable to see them.

Especially, with regard to the Chemmani mass graves - you have unearthed a certain number of skeletons - there has been no forensic analysis or anything; you have not done anything. Unearthing further skeletons is another matter, but with regard to the skeletons or the remains found so far, what have you all done?

And, one last matter I would like to add, Hon. Minister, is the issue of political prisoners. The previous Government under President Ranil Wickremesinghe used to engage with us and even though we did not accept the TRC, they, at least, had a proposal. When we demanded the release of political prisoners, they released, at least, one or two. Actually, President Ranil Wickremesinghe released a few. But, you all have not released any. You are not even agreeing that there are political prisoners.

So, in light of this situation, Hon. Minister, I would like to have answers to my Question.

Thank you.

ගු නීතිඥ තර්තෂානණ නානායක්කාර මතතා (අධිකරණ සත ජාතික ඒකාබද්ධාතා අමාතයුමා) (மொண்புமிகு சட்டத்தரணி ஹர்ஷன நொணொயக்கொர - நீதி மற்றும் யதசிய ஒருமமப்பொட்டு அமமச்சர்) (The Hon. Harshana Nanayakkara, Attorney-at-Law - Minister of Justice and National Integration) Hon. Speaker, first of all, I would like to thank the Hon. Shanakiyan Rasamanickam for raising this most pertinent Question. He is one of the very few Members who periodically raise these questions, which actually bring the Government also into accountability. So, thank you for this timely and thoughtful Question. Despite the short duration, I have also prepared thoroughly to answer because I understand that the Question is important and again, I am very grateful that you raised it. As you know, although we have been in power for the last one-and-a-half years, we have to work with records and databases that existed prior to us even contemplating coming here.

1. The Government has acknowledged that a significant number of persons remain unaccounted for following the final stages of the armed conflict in 2009. Official figures have varied over time, reflecting different sources and methodologies such as Presidential Commissions of Inquiry, the Lessons Learnt and Reconciliation Commission and the Office on Missing Persons, which only

started in 2018, a number of years after the war. So, we may not necessarily agree with the number here, but according to the records, this is all I have. I do not know whether it is right or wrong and whether anyone would agree that they are it is right or wrong. But, I can only give you what is on record from 2009. So, the number I can come up with is, 2,764. That could be debated, which I would not go against in the case of a debate.

2. Then, a credible consolidated database. Hon. Member, it is very correct that that has been a massive challenge. From the time we came in, we were working with the ICRC in developing this database. So, let me answer you on that question. The Government accepts that the absence of a fully credible, consolidated National Database has been a longstanding challenge. Fragmentation across multiple institutions such as the OMP, the Human Rights Commission and prior to that, ad hoc commissions which have been going on for a very long time has hindered the creation of a unified record. However, the following steps are currently being taken by the Office on Missing Persons:

• Information from different sources has been consolidated and entered into a temporary database to ensure that existing records are preserved and accessible.

• A formal proposal has been developed for the establishment of a comprehensive Database Management System - DBMS - to serve as the long-term solution.

In these instances, we might get help from our international friends to assist us with that.

• A requirement identification process with relevant stakeholders is currently underway to ensure that the system is designed to meet humanitarian, legal and accountability needs.

• In parallel, the Office on Missing Persons is being strengthened to centralize data collection, coordinate with law enforcement and civil registries and introduce digital platforms to improve transparency and accuracy.

• Engagement with international partners and civil society continues to ensure methodological rigor and public trust in the process.

I must say that I myself was involved with Troika as well as the UN Resident Coordinator on Human Rights. The moment we develop these proposals, they would be given to them, and we are working on them.

3. About the Chemmani mass graves, I am glad that you asked because there is a lot of misinformation doing in the media, saying that the Government is not releasing enough funds or things of that nature, which are not correct at all. Even last time, we had released Rs. 19 million and as you are aware, the

2523 2524

පාර්ලිගේන්තතුව

graves were discovered on the 19th of February, 2025 and currently, that site is protected by civil community groups as well as the Police, jointly, so that evidence does not get damaged. If you take a certified copy of the court record, it would be seen that Police and the local community are jointly protecting this, and as I told you, the Ministry of Justice and National Integration has given the budget. We are using a lot of technologies at the moment to minimize the risk of damaging potential evidence and to ensure that the process adheres to international best practices and standard operating procedures - SOPs - in forensic archaeology and human rights investigations. The ultimate objective is to ensure a scientifically rigorous and transparent excavation process that upholds judicial inquiry and delivers justice to the families of the victims. The means and methods and the timeline are actually decided by the court house together with forensic anthropologists and archaeologists who are working on the sites, JMOs and police and crime scene investigators in accordance with international protocols.

Let me add something else which is not here. That is, we are also trying to prepare a timeline to bring a closure to this, and once the remains are gathered, the court will direct us to send them for forensic investigations. Some investigations are being done for identification - a public identification process went through - and I will give you more updates as they come.

4 & 5. I will respond to the questions collectively since

both are similar.

The Office on Missing Persons has reported information relating to 19 mass grave sites and it is currently working closely with eight sites located in Colombo, Mullaitivu, Mannar, Batticaloa and Trincomalee. Overall, the OMP plays a critical role in advancing its mandate by expanding its operations. For this, technical support was obtained from the ICRC through workshops on forensic archaeology and engaging in training more than 40 excavation officers from the Department of Archaeology, who were trained on mass grave investigations. An extension of the workshop on advanced forensic archaeology was also organized with 60 participants from the Department of Archaeology. This training and technical support was provided to the expert team in order to create a pool of Excavation Officers to investigate the mass graves and to accelerate the process in finding out the fate of the missing persons. The OMP is working closely with the ICRC and there are commitments established to work collaboratively for the next three years. So, we are working with them.

6. Hon. Rasamanickam, let us accept the fact that these are very painful histories involving victims and very painful memories for all parties concerned. War is no game; war, we do not want

to repeat. However, we have to accept the fact that during a period of war, people are made to disappear by both sides. It is not right, but wrong, and we stand against it. But, that is the nature of war. However, we do not justify it.

Currently, there are a couple of court cases going on with regard to specific disappearances and let them take their due course because the law does not look at what is right or wrong in the eyes of a war, but what is right and wrong according to law. So, if someone had surrendered to the Army and had gone missing, then courts must investigate under the legal process. Therefore, the court process is ongoing. However, since you brought this to my notice as well, I would invite you to, if there are any specific instances where, still, there are blocks in the system or some things have not been brought before courts under the legal process, please indicate them, too. I am quite willing to sit down on a separate conversation with you and attend to it.

7. The question is with regard to the Sri Lanka Accountability Project - SLAP. As far as that is concerned -that came up in January and it is a unilateral publication on the part of the UN - soon, a statement will be made by the UN Permanent Representative to Geneva.

But, my response for the time being is this. Sri Lanka has not accepted the Sri Lanka Accountability Project as it constitutes an external initiative that is counterproductive to the ongoing domestic reconciliation processes. Therefore, the Government will not engage with that external mechanism.

Addressing the 60th Session of the Human Rights Council in September 2025, the Hon. Minister of Foreign Affairs, Foreign Employment and Tourism stated, “The Government is opposed to any external mechanism imposed on us such as the SLAP, which will only serve to create divisions, thereby jeopardizing the genuine and tangible national processes that have already been set in motion.”

Also, if I add a few things - this will be officially communicated in time to come - how they have unilaterally gathered facts and the methodologies used are something that we cannot accept, and we have maintained it to be that way.

8. Since assuming office, the Government has supported and strengthened the work of the Office on Missing Persons - OMP- resulting in several tangible outcomes:

First and foremost, Hon. Member, you and I both know that, although the Office on Missing Persons came into being in 2018, for any of these mechanisms to function, there must be a proper political will and I still maintain that despite the shortcomings of the staff as such, the political will had not been there from 2019 until we came into power. So, from the time we came into power, the first challenge was manpower. Now, we have

2525 2526

[ගරු නීතිඥ හර්ලෂය නානායක්ෂකාර මහ ා]

2026 මාර්ලතු 20

recruited nearly 100 people to carry out the work of the OMP and the OR. And, they were extremely short of staff and was not functional. Now, we have also allocated them money. So, now what has happened? Cabinet Approval has been granted to extend the OMP’s mandate to inquire cases beyond the year 2000 and newly reported cases, too. So, now, there are more cases being reported and the numbers are going up.

Also, I must say that by the Cabinet, Rs. 375 million was allocated for the OMP’s inquiry panel of 75 people. It is now carrying out inquiries into complaints on missing persons and we are intending to complete 5,000 complaints this year and the remaining 6,000 next year. So, within two years, we are hoping that these panels will be able to finish investigations into those complaints and I have told them separately and in person also to keep in mind when they are doing it that this is not just about paying money and but about finding truth and justice for the people. Therefore, if there are any perpetrators being identified in this process, relevant steps would be taken to alert the other official authorities to carry out the necessary measures. When it comes to the mass graves, the OMP is acting as an observer in eight ongoing cases and is assisting the court in different ways, including assisting in coordination between different agencies while making interventions. The OMP has been involved in supporting on technical capacity and has provided training for archaeologists (focusing on forensic archaeology - two trainings in 2025) and for JMOs and odontologists (Focusing on age techniques for sub-adults in 2025.) With regard to database development, different sources of information have been consolidated into a temporary database. A formal proposal has been developed for a comprehensive Database Management System, as I told you earlier. And also, the Government has allocated a dedicated budget for the initiative. A requirement of identification process with stakeholders is underway to ensure the system meets humanitarian, legal and accountability needs. With regard to supporting families, the OMP has expedited the issuance of Certificates of Absence, enabling families to access interim relief and manage legal/administrative matters. Continued engagement with families and civil society has been prioritized to ensure credibility and transparency in all processes.

With regard to regional outreach, the OMP continues to operate regional offices in Jaffna, Batticaloa, Mannar, Matara and Killinochchi. In 2025, the OMP set up a sub-office in Mullaitivu to ensure access.

9. The Office for Reparations was established in 2018 under Act, No. 34 of 2018, succeeding the

Rehabilitation of Persons, Properties and Industries Authority Act, No. 29 of 1987. That is the legal background.

Reparations are provided aiming at individuals as well as through collective mechanisms guided by policies and frameworks.

Develop an OR Act through following thematic areas of interventions: compensation and financial support, livelihood support and psychosocial support.

The Government of Sri Lanka continues to extend support to families of missing persons through the reparation framework administered by OR. Monetary grants referred to as revival payments are disbursed to dependents of missing persons based on recommendation from Office on Missing Persons in accordance with Section 11(a) Part II of the Act.

As of 31st December, 2025, a total of 4,456 claimants have received final revival payments. This process ensures that financial assistance is delivered in a transparent and an accountable manner. During the period 2003-2005, a total of 31 collective reparation initiatives were implemented across multiple districts in Northern and Eastern Provinces. These programmes benefitted 1,523 conflict-affected individuals including 1,021 women, 532 men, 231 students with disabilities and 211 girls. All initiatives were developed through participatory process involving consulting with affected communities, community-based organizations, District and Divisional Secretaries and the Department of Social Services under the Ministry of Women and Child Affairs.

Now, just to add a small thing, I do accept the fact that last year, the Office for Reparations has not been able to function to its optimum because the Office on Missing Persons’ inquiries were a little slow; they were not moving through because of lack of staff. So, because of that, the OR was not going as fast as it ought to have, but this year that problem has been solved. At the same time, we are preparing a package for missing persons, not just money, but a complete social package, a one-off, where they would get psychosocial support, other livelihood support and recognition as a special category of victims. So, that much, we are doing.

10. The Government is committed to advancing accountability through credible domestic processes.

The Government’s commitment to advancing reconciliation, human rights and well-being of all Sri Lankans arises from our genuine belief in the fundamental importance of human rights and the need to ensure the rights and well-being of our own people. The Government has the mandate and is firmly and sincerely committed to working towards a country that respects and celebrates the diversity of its people with no division or discrimination based on race, religion, class and caste. The Government is resolved not to leave

2527 2528

පාර්ලිගේන්තතුව

room for resurgence of racism or extremism in Sri Lanka. As Sri Lanka moves forward on this path through its domestic processes, the understanding, encouragement and support of the international community is welcome and appreciated.

Let me say just a few lines to finish off. Once again, I would like to thank you, Hon. Member, for raising all these questions. As much as we are pushing, I would also prefer things to happen a little faster. But, believe me, we are on the correct direction. And, wherever we are having shortcomings, Hon. Member, I am very glad and very happy to engage with you.

Especially with regard to prisoners, prison overcrowding and those issues, right now, a Committee is working on it, and soon, we will be able to sort out most of them.

I once again thank you very sincerely for the Question raised.

ගු නීතිඥ තර්තෂානණ නානායක්කාර මතතා (மொண்புமிகு சட்டத்தரணி ஹர்ஷன நொணொயக்கொர) (The Hon. Harshana Nanayakkara, Attorney-at-Law) Thank you, Hon. Member. I do understand that victims need justice and justice is what our Government is going to give. When it comes to justice for people, the families of missing persons, we do not divide on North, East or South; it is “One country, one people”. With regard to the issue of missing persons, a closure is necessary, justice is necessary. Whether one year and four months is sufficient for that is another matter altogether, but we are moving in the right direction and are on the right path.

Secondly, regarding persons who went missing towards the end of the war in 2009, obviously, I could sit and argue, but it is not going to serve any purpose because we do not know all the details. But, we understand the pain of it and we are working on it.

Then, the members of the Office for Reparations are appointed by the Constitutional Council based on the applications received. I will, however, take up your concerns at appropriate forums.

Also, I would like to say that we went through the Commission for Truth, Unity and Reconciliation in Sri Lanka Act - the CTUR Act - but there is a problem. It was hastily drafted during the previous regime and some Sections of it are conflicting with the OR Act and the OMP Act, which are very powerful Acts. So, right now, I have sent that Act to the Law Commission to find a suitable way of getting it passed through.

Further, I would like to say that causing a person to go missing is a crime; killing is a crime. But, whatever the crime is, the local criminal law will be applied equally to everybody who had committed it. If new laws are necessary, they will be brought in.

Finally, when it comes to international mechanisms, right now, we are working to get expertise, help, training or whatever that is necessary for those. And, believe me, when it comes to their enforcement, within the next three or four years, you would see that we would do a fair job. We are committed to doing a fair and a decent job to address the grievances of the people.

Once again, Hon. Member, I thank you very sincerely for raising this Question.

ගු කථානායකුමා (மொண்புமிகு சபொநொயகர் அெர்கள்) (The Hon. Speaker) ‍රධාන වතයකටයුතු ආරේභග ජ ගයෝයනා.
